# PS4 Orbis Wine port handoff — 2026-10-14

## Current handoff (updated 2026-10-14)

Space Cadet Pinball is the known-good baseline. The current experimental
target is the GOG copy of Oblivion, to test support for non-relocatable images.

### Hard rule for Oblivion test packages (2026-10-15)

Always build the Oblivion test title with `scripts/build-relocatable-wine-ps4.sh`.
Do not use `scripts/build-app-ps4.sh` directly: its low-base eboot occupies
`0x400000`, colliding with Oblivion.exe's preferred base because the game has
stripped PE relocations. That produces `failed to create main module ...
status c0000018` before input or graphics are exercised. The regular build was
accidentally installed on 2026-10-15 and reproduced that known failure; it was
not useful test data.

The corrected diagnostic package was rebuilt with the high-address Wine PRX,
keeping title ID `GLDS10056` and the Vulkan instance checkpoints, then uploaded
to `/data/pkg/wine-input-fix-test.pkg` (FTP returned `226`). It is
`pkgout/wine-input-fix-test-reloc/IV0000-GLDS10056_00-GLDS100560000000.pkg`,
SHA256 `bdc81f7ff25aafa99cc9a46887027334b092a040cf966252ef348c87a615fe70`.
The staged package contains `sce_module/relocated_wine_app.prx`, and its
`win32u.prx` contains all `PS4 Vulkan instance phase=` markers. Install this
package before the next Oblivion run. Future Oblivion packages must use the
relocatable build path even when only Wine diagnostics changed.

### Oblivion relocation experiment (2026-10-14)

Use the distinct-title packages so the working packages remain installed:

- `GLDS10044` unmapped all three low eboot segments before entering the Wine
  main. It immediately SIGSEGVed in `sceVideoOutOpen`, reading the unmapped
  eboot data address `0x4a0000`.
- `GLDS10045` kept the eboot mapped through launcher/display setup and delayed
  unmapping until the selected game was about to start. The launcher ran and
  selected `oblivion-gog`, but the handoff stub discarded `argc`/`argv`. The
  restarted process therefore came back with `argc=0` and showed the launcher
  again; Oblivion itself did not start.
- `GLDS10046` forwards `argc`/`argv` from the eboot stub to the high-address
  PRX and then to Wine's main. It is built and uploaded as
  `/data/pkg/oblivion-relocatable.pkg`. It installed, restarted into the game
  process, and loaded `C:\Games\OblivionGOG\Oblivion.exe`, so the delayed
  eboot release and argument forwarding work far enough to reach the title.
  The app then crashed about 16 seconds after the executable loaded.
- The runtime log reports a syscall fault in `ntdll.prx+0xbf91b`, with an
  invalid read near `0xffffffff0`. That offset resolves to the diagnostic
  stack walk in patch `0876`, which reads `context->Rsp[i]` without checking
  that the address is mapped. The klog's fatal report has a long repeating
  stack, but the original exception is not yet identified. Treat this as a
  likely diagnostic-induced crash, not proof that Oblivion itself crashed for
  this reason.
- `0876` has been revised to retain the fault-register report but omit that
  raw stack walk. The modified ntdll compiled and was linked into the PS4 PRX.
  A fresh package, `GLDS10047` ("Oblivion Relocatable Test 2"), is built locally
  at `pkgout/oblivion-relocatable-2/IV0000-GLDS10047_00-GLDS100470000000.pkg`;
  SHA256 is `4779e76d72b7a495622e9e12f9c38b4c27e11493add1ac3c3c50009e2699ea61`.
  It was uploaded successfully to `/data/pkg/oblivion-relocatable-2.pkg`
  (FTP returned `226`). Install it and choose `oblivion-gog` for the next run;
  the goal is to reveal the original fault without the diagnostic crashing
  while it scans the stack.

### GLDS10047 runtime retest and resolution A/B (2026-10-14)

The user installed GLDS10047 and launched `oblivion-gog`. Wine loaded
`C:\Games\OblivionGOG\Oblivion.exe` and stayed alive for about two minutes;
there was no SIGSEGV, syscall fault, or crash. The user saw the game intro /
loading screen and then closed the app from the PS4 menu. Klog confirms a
normal shell suspend/kill at 17:31:39, not a title crash.

The unresolved runtime symptom is presentation: the logger recorded only five
frames submitted and one shown, then `VK_SUBOPTIMAL_KHR` and swapchain
recreation every ~65 ms. The reported game output became 1440x1080 while the
Vulkan swapchain stayed 1920x1080. The installed profile is otherwise set to a
1920x1080 desktop with `scaling=fit`; it omits `view`, so the default is
`window`.

The prefix INI explains the 4:3 render mode: before the next run,
`bFull Screen=1`, `iSize W=640`, `iSize H=480`. I changed only the prefix's
`Documents/My Games/Oblivion/Oblivion.ini` to 1920x1080, preserving fullscreen.
The updated INI was uploaded to
`/data/prospero-win/prefixes/oblivion-gog/drive_c/users/prospero/Documents/My Games/Oblivion/Oblivion.ini`
and verified byte-for-byte (SHA256
`8d65a62868552a7321f796c1ffd47390784fb43fc7aa30320d8b38286a5bae24`). The
original 640x480 INI is saved locally as `/tmp/Oblivion.ini.original` for
rollback. No package rebuild or reinstall is needed for this next A/B run.

The next launch used that INI: the user confirms the window is now positioned
normally, and telemetry reports 1920x1080 frames. There are no memory faults.
The user hears audio on the initial menu, but not on the selection screen.
Audio callbacks continue while the non-silent sample counter stays at 3666,
so the later screen is sending silent buffers rather than losing the audio
transport. Controller reads work: the log captured D-pad down/right/up
presses, but the `oblivion-gog` profile had `mode=keyboard` and no bindings,
so no game input was posted (`inputs=0`).

I updated the remote profile with `up/down/left/right` mapped to those keys,
Cross to Enter, and Circle/Options to Escape. It was verified byte-for-byte
(SHA256 `b9b44c3b33b4657e73c4c4124e59ca11cedc916879460eb1e90b8426034ff19e`).
The current run loaded the old profile, so close it from the PS4 menu and
relaunch `oblivion-gog` to test the bindings. No package rebuild is needed.

### Oblivion pointer and joystick A/B

On the next run, the user reports Start/Cross now works, but the selection
screen needs a pointer and button presses still lag. Telemetry confirms the
new keyboard bindings are delivered: D-pad directions arrive as arrow keys,
Cross as Enter, and Circle as Escape; `inputs` advances and `refused=0`.
The profile has no native XInput mode, so this is deliberate keyboard input.

The INI already had `bUse Joystick=1`. Bethesda's keyboard troubleshooting
recommends setting it to `0` when joystick input interferes
([Bethesda support](https://help.bethesda.net/app/answers/detail/a_id/17842/~/what-do-i-do-if-im-having-issues-with-my-keyboard-in-the-elder-scrolls-iv/)).
For the next run I set that to `0` and added `mouse=right_stick` plus
`mouse_speed=1200` to the Oblivion profile; the existing D-pad and face-button
keyboard bindings remain. Both remote files were verified byte-for-byte.
Rollback copies are `/tmp/Oblivion.ini.1920-joystick1` and
`/tmp/oblivion-gog.profile.original`. Since the current game process loaded
the old settings, close it and relaunch to test the right-stick pointer and
whether disabling the game's joystick path removes the button lag.

The user then confirmed the right stick moves the mouse but there is no click.
I added `r2=mouse_left` while retaining `cross=enter` and the pointer binding;
the updated profile was uploaded and verified byte-for-byte (SHA256
`394045b3cb9121d0ef554466397e857b2dce4d16a28873c461a36e89dd70ee8d`). This
profile change applies on the next launch. Test R2 to click; no package rebuild
is needed.

The GLDS10046 klog is `build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261014-164221.log`; runtime

### Oblivion input trace (GLDS10047, 2026-10-14)

On the current menu run, the user pressed L1 once. The active profile maps L1
to Enter. Runtime confirms the event reached the foreground Oblivion window
`0x10030` as `VK_RETURN` down/up through `ps5_ProcessEvents`, both with status
0. Native input counters show two accepted events and zero refused. This
proves the launcher-to-Wine input path, but not that Oblivion's message loop
or menu accepted the key. The menu remained stuck and the output stayed at
four submitted frames.

CPU samples put guest thread `0x0024` at `ucrtbase.dll` RVA `0x168ce`, which
resolves to `_sse2_memmove+0x52`; this is a generic memory-copy routine, not
an input handler, and does not yet explain the stall. The run logs are
`build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261014-172725.log` and
`build-ps4-logs/ps5log-20261014-172729.log`.

The profile now enables `winedebug = +ps5drv,+msg`. On the subsequent L1
press, Wine again accepted VK_RETURN down/up with status 0. The `+msg` trace
shows the driver's WM_INPUT and its internal WM_USER wake, and thread `0x0024`
dequeued that wake; it shows no WM_KEYDOWN/WM_KEYUP reaching the game window.
Frames remained at four. This narrows the failure to hardware-key queueing or
delivery after `NtUserSendHardwareInput`; the game still has not demonstrated
that it received Enter. Logs remain in the same klog and ps5log files listed
above. Keep both listeners running for the next targeted queue trace.

The user then pressed Cross (profile maps it to left mouse). Runtime saw the
mouse-button down/up packets, but no `WM_LBUTTONDOWN`/`WM_LBUTTONUP` appeared
in `+msg`; cursor-clip messages and the driver's WM_USER wake did appear. The
user first saw the menu advance to “Advanced,” then the next click did nothing
and the screen appeared stuck. Frame count remained four throughout; process
CPU continued rising. The user closed it from the PS4 menu at 18:21:39, and
klog confirms a normal suspend/kill, no crash. Treat input delivery and the
stalled render loop as potentially coupled but distinct questions. Next
diagnostic should instrument the server-side hardware-message queue and
identify why frame submission stopped after the fourth frame; don't spend
another run cycling menu bindings.

The missing window decorations are resolved. They were never a painting
failure: `ps5drv` composed every window into one screen buffer and let a
window that hid, moved or closed clear its whole visible rectangle, erasing
what the windows above it had painted. Explorer's desktop window is the size
of the desktop, so tearing it down wiped the entire screen three milliseconds
after the game window first painted. Patch `0902` clears only the parts no
other window owns; `0901` adds the z order it needs and separately stops a
lower window overdrawing a higher one on flush. Confirmed on hardware in
`ORBW00023` and visually by the user. `ORBW00024` carries the same fix without
the investigation traces. The user has confirmed that this package works
and Pinball looks correct; treat it as the known-good package for the Pinball
baseline.

The decorations themselves were then judged not to belong on the console: the
presentation is effectively full screen, so a Windows title bar in it is not
wanted. See the section below before reopening that.

The current `ORBW00020` split runtime can launch Pinball and the established
GTA IV profile. Pinball produced frames and audio, and GTA IV loaded into the
game. These runs establish that the package can execute those known-good
paths, not that either title is fully correct. The separate `gtaiv-test`
profile run exited back to the launcher before producing frames or audio.
Fallout New Vegas reached its familiar three-frame hang; this is not a new
result. Do not use those diagnostic package runs as evidence that the title
issues are fixed.

### Pinball decorations and the composition defect (2026-10-14, resolved)

Two separate things were tangled together here. The composition defect is
fixed and confirmed. Whether decorations should be drawn at all is a
presentation decision the user has made: they should not.

Wine paints the decorations correctly and they do reach the screen: both
captured videos show the window fully decorated -- blue caption, system menu
icon, white border -- for exactly one frame (`IMG_7992` at ~17.2 s,
`IMG_7994` at ~22.3 s). The next frame the whole window is black, splash
content included, and about two seconds later only the client comes back.

The cause is in `ps5drv`, and it is not about decorations. Explorer's desktop
window `1002a` is hidden and destroyed three milliseconds after the game
window paints:

    09:24:59.173  window_surface_flush Flushing hwnd 10046 ... dirty (0,0)-(608,467)
    09:24:59.176  user_destroy_window (1002a)
    09:24:59.176  dump_winpos_flags flags: ... SWP_HIDEWINDOW
    09:24:59.179  destroy_window 1002a
    09:25:01.183  window_surface_flush Flushing hwnd 10046 ... bounds (556,200)-(571,222)

`ps5_WindowPosChanged()` and `ps5_DestroyWindow()` both called
`clear_screen_rect( &window->visible )`, and the desktop window's visible rect
is the whole 800x600 screen, so that one call blanked everything the game
window had painted. Nothing repaints a non-client area afterwards, so the
caption and borders never returned while the client did.

The user then ran Pinball on ordinary Wine for comparison and concluded the
decorations do not belong on the console at all: the PS4 presentation is
effectively full screen, so a Windows title bar floating in it is not wanted.
The user now confirms Pinball looks correct in the fixed package. Treat
"Pinball has no window decorations" as closed and not a defect. Note
that Wine still computes and paints a 48-pixel non-client band (3-pixel
border, 26-pixel caption, 19-pixel menu bar) and a 606x467 window on the
800x600 desktop, so if the decorations are genuinely unwanted the window
should be made borderless and sized to the desktop rather than left painted
and then overwritten. `ps5drv` also already supports `view = window`, which
presents the bounding box of the visible top-level windows scaled to the TV
instead of the whole desktop; the profile currently uses `view = desktop`,
which is why the table sits in a black field.

What remains a genuine defect, independent of decorations, is that a window
blanked pixels owned by the windows above it. `ORBW00023` fixes that and is
uploaded; it removes the two-second black window and the corner overwrite
described below. Any window that hides, moves or closes hit this.

### Pinball decoration investigation (superseded, kept for the evidence)

The section above is the resolution. This one records the first pass, whose
conclusion was incomplete: it found the desktop window's corner blit and took
it for the cause. It is not. The corner blit is real and `0901` fixes it, but
the decorations were being erased by a full-screen clear, not overdrawn.
`ORBW00021`, built on this diagnosis alone, changed nothing visible.

The current profile is `src/profiles/pinball.profile`: 800x600 desktop,
`view = desktop`, GDI, and a dedicated prefix.

The non-client pixels were followed end to end. They are painted and they do
reach the screen.

`nc_paint()` runs for the Pinball window and draws the frame. Its window is
at (97,44)-(703,511) on the 800x600 desktop, 606x467 with a 3-pixel border
and a 48-pixel top non-client area (26-pixel caption plus a 19-pixel menu
bar), and the client area is the 600x416 the game asked for:

    08:46:53.077  nc_paint 10046 4
    08:46:53.077  nc_paint drawing menu with rect (3,29)-(603,48)
    08:46:53.093  window_surface_flush Flushing hwnd 10046,
                    surface (0,0)-(640,512), bounds (0,0)-(606,467),
                    dirty (0,0)-(608,467)

That flush covers the whole window, the caption and borders included, and
`ps5_surface_flush()` copies it into the screen buffer.

Two milliseconds later explorer's desktop window `1002a`, which is below it
in the z order, repaints a corner of its own background:

    08:46:53.095  window_surface_flush Flushing hwnd 1002a,
                    surface (0,0)-(896,640), bounds (0,0)-(240,189),
                    dirty (0,0)-(240,189)

`ps5_surface_flush()` blitted every window's dirty rectangle into the one
screen buffer with no z order and no occlusion: `windows[]` was in creation
order and nothing clipped a lower window against a higher one. So that blit
overwrites screen (97,44)-(240,189) -- the game window's left border, the
left third of its caption and its system menu. The playfield returns because
the game repaints it every frame; the frame is never repainted, so the damage
is permanent. The same two-flush sequence appears in every Pinball run in
`ps5log-20261014-live.log` (16 occurrences).

The window surface clip region win32u hands the driver does not solve this.
`ps5_surface_set_clip()` is a no-op, but honouring it would not help: the
server's `get_surface_region()` computes that region from the window's own
shape and its child windows that own a pixel format, not from the windows
above it. A driver whose windows are real windows gets its z order from the
display server; `ps5drv` has none, so it has to track the order itself.

Patch `0901-win32u-ps5-compose-windows-in-z-order` does that. `update_z_order()`
sorts `windows[]` into the desktop's z order, topmost first, from
`list_window_children( get_desktop_window() )` whenever a window position
changes; `ps5_DestroyWindow()` now closes the gap with `memmove` instead of
swapping the last entry into it; and `ps5_surface_flush()` clips its dirty
rectangle to the parts the windows above it leave visible.

This accounts for the window's top-left corner with certainty. Whether the
remaining caption was also absent for a second reason is not decidable from
the logs: only the hardware test separates "the corner is repaired and the
caption is whole" from "the corner is repaired and the rest is still blank".
Judge `ORBW00021` on that, along with the already-working input, frames and
audio.

A related gap is left in place deliberately, because Pinball never moves its
window: when a window moves or hides, `ps5_WindowPosChanged()` still calls
`clear_screen_rect()` on the area it vacated, blanking it rather than asking
the windows below to repaint. Now that the driver knows the z order, that
clear could invalidate the windows underneath instead.

The console is online. Leave the existing klog and `ps5logd` listeners running
while testing; Wine diagnostics require both. Use direct GoldHEN FTP with
`scripts/ftp-put.py` and no login. Do not probe or list FTP unnecessarily:
the service has crashed during earlier probes. The current captures are
`build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261014-054755.log` and
`build-ps4-logs/ps5log-20261014-live.log`.

`ORBW00019` ran successfully. This confirms the `wow64.dll` repair in the
known-good runtime. The complete host PE runtime is rebuilt and `ORBW00020`
has now run Pinball for about a minute: 29,861 frame submissions, 2,554 shown
frames, and 3,605 audible buffers, with no early stack overflow or process
signal. `ORBW00018` is a
conclusive single-file reproduction:
it uses the working EBOOT, PRXs, and all other PE files, changes only
`win/wine/lib/wine/x86_64-windows/wow64.dll`, and crashes with the same
`virtual_setup_exception stack overflow 384 bytes` as the full split runtime.
The rebuilt DLL imports `kernel32.dll` and `ntdll.dll`; the working DLL imports
only `ntdll.dll` (verified with `x86_64-w64-mingw32-objdump -p`). Patch
`wine/orbis/base-patches/0613-wow64-avoid-kernel32-debug-helper.patch` now
removes those debug formatting calls in `dlls/wow64/syscall.c`, avoiding the
new `kernel32.dll` dependency. The rebuilt DLL imports only `ntdll.dll` and
its package passed all 1,781 payload hash checks. The full host PE runtime has
since been rebuilt, and uploaded package `ORBW00020` tests all matching rebuilt
PE files in the coherent split package. The rebuilt PE `ntdll.dll`
pair (`ORBW00013`) and `wowprospero.dll` (`ORBW00014`) both worked alone.
`ORBW00015` also ran successfully with those three combined: at 07:21:33 it
had 3,902 frame puts and 1,031 audible buffers. `ORBW00016` (all x86_64 PE)
and `ORBW00017` (all i386 PE) remain uploaded but untested; they are lower
priority while `ORBW00019` tests the isolated repair.

Do **not** use `ORBW00007` or `ORBW00008` as test results. Both failed in the
PS4 loader with `PRX_SCE_MODULE_LOAD_ERROR` before Wine started. Their ad hoc
packages omitted `sce_module/libc.prx` and `libSceFios2.prx`; `ORBW00009/10`
include byte-identical copies from the working package. The normal split
`tools/build_app_orbis.sh` already requires both. New one-off A/B packages
should use `scripts/repackage-orbis-ab.py`, which rejects a base stage missing
either module and verifies all staged payload hashes.

## Results already measured

- `ORBW00002` (split EBOOT, known-good Wine runtime) ran Pinball: first game
  frame at roughly 11 seconds, 2,895 submitted frames and 1,215 audible
  buffers by about 22 seconds. The split EBOOT is sound.
- `ORBW00004` (split PRXs, known-good EBOOT/PE) and `ORBW00006` (PRXs rebuilt
  with FreeType/Vulkan sonames, known-good EBOOT/PE) both loaded Pinball, but
  first frame took roughly 82–85 seconds. Both then reached thousands of
  frames and audible buffers. FreeType was a real configure gap but did not
  explain the long startup.
- Both split PRX runs included patch `0900-server-log-pollfds-before-poll`,
  which writes before every wineserver `poll()`. Their klogs contain about
  1,200 such lines before first frame. The patch is now outside the normal
  series at `wine/orbis/diagnostic-patches`; `ORBW00011` tests whether removing
  it restores startup speed.
- `ORBW00005` (old split PE only) and `ORBW00003` (old split full runtime)
  failed with the same early Wine stack-overflow message. A stamped host PE
  rebuild completed after fixing a missing generated Vulkan allocator helper;
  `ORBW00009` still failed about 1.5 seconds after Wine game boot with
  `virtual_setup_exception stack overflow 384 bytes`, followed by PS4 SIGSYS.
  Stale PE build output is therefore not the cause. `ORBW00012` later showed
  the same failure with matching rebuilt PRXs, and `ORBW00018` isolated the
  rebuilt PE `wow64.dll` as a sufficient trigger.
- `ORBW00011` changed only `wineserver.prx` from `ORBW00006` and reached its
  first Pinball frame in about 11 seconds, with audio by about 13 seconds.
  This confirms the per-poll logger caused the 82–85-second startup.
- `ORBW00012` connected to wineserver, then logged the identical
  `virtual_setup_exception stack overflow 384 bytes` at 07:06:13.652,
  approximately 1.6 seconds after game boot, followed by SIGSYS. `ORBW00010`
  logged the same stack overflow at 07:06:49.245, approximately 6.4 seconds
  after game boot, followed by SIGILL. The slower `ORBW00010` timing is
  consistent with its per-poll logger. The failure survives a coherent PE/PRX
  rebuild and removal of the logger.
- `ORBW00013` reached 1,319 Pinball frame puts and 1,219 audible buffers by
  about 20 seconds. `ORBW00014` reached at least 523 frame puts and 801 audible
  buffers by about 18 seconds. Neither isolated PE swap reproduced the early
  stack overflow.
- `ORBW00018` connected to wineserver at 07:17:56.008, then logged the exact
  `virtual_setup_exception stack overflow 384 bytes addr 0 stack
  1000123e80 (1000120000-1000124000-100021fd20)` at 07:17:57.074. Klog
  recorded SIGSYS at 07:17:57.212 and a coredump for title `ORBW00018`.
  This isolates a sufficient trigger to the rebuilt PE `wow64.dll` itself.
- `ORBW00015` combines rebuilt i386/x86_64 `ntdll.dll` and x86_64
  `wowprospero.dll`, leaving working `wow64.dll` in place. It ran Pinball and
  reached 3,902 frame puts and 1,031 audible buffers at 07:21:33. This confirms
  those three rebuilt PE modules are mutually compatible when the working
  `wow64.dll` is retained.

  Wine's `debugstr_w()` helper in backend selection diagnostics. Its PE import

  `490f6d5dcbb2a5047345b8af88d114bbcaad69a8` with the corrected `0613` patch.
  The first clean build attempt exposed a malformed hunk and a stale expected

  The build completed at four jobs after eight exceeded available memory. The

- `ORBW00020` replaces all 1,598 package PE files with matching rebuilt host

## Build and deployment pointers

- Working A/B base: `pkgout/orbis-no-poll-log/pkg-stage` (`ORBW00011`).
  Guarded builder: `scripts/repackage-orbis-ab.py --base-stage ... --out-dir
  ... --title-id ORBW000xx --title ... --replace REL=SOURCE`; it checks both
  required platform PRXs and verifies every staged payload hash. Use a fresh
  title ID for each test. `ORBW00013` through `ORBW00020` were built this way.
- Orbis PRX rebuilds (`win32u.prx` and the rest of `sce_module/`) use
  `scripts/orbis-wine-prx-env.sh`, then the split checkout's
  `tools/build_wine_orbis_prx.sh`. The PRX tree is
  `.deps/wine-orbis-full-legacy`, **not** `.deps/wine-orbis`: the working
  packages' PRXs come from the legacy tree, and the two trees produce
  differently sized modules. A full PRX rebuild takes a few minutes and only
  the changed module differs, so a `win32u.prx`-only change needs no PE
  rebuild.
- Rebuilt host PE install: `$SCRATCH_ROOT/prospero-win-orbis-upstream/.deps/wine-orbis-host/install/usr/lib/wine`.
  Patched Wine source and build are siblings `source` and `build` beneath
  `.deps/wine-orbis-host`. The pin is Wine commit
  `490f6d5dcbb2a5047345b8af88d114bbcaad69a8`.
- `wow64.dll` source of the import was the `debugstr_w()` calls in
  `.deps/wine-orbis-host/source/dlls/wow64/syscall.c` around lines 933–943.
  Its `syscall.o` referenced `__imp_IsBadStringPtrW`; old patch `0613` added
  kernel32 to `dlls/wow64/Makefile.in`. The patch now avoids that helper and
  the new DLL imports only `ntdll.dll`. `ORBW00019` confirms this repair on

- Use `source scripts/common.sh; need_console; python3 scripts/ftp-put.py
  "$ORBIS_CONSOLE" "$ORBIS_FTP_PORT" LOCAL.pkg /data/pkg/NAME.pkg` for upload.
  GoldHEN FTP accepts direct `TYPE I`, `PASV`, `STOR` without login. Do not
  probe/list FTP unnecessarily. The current listener log paths are at the top
  of this handoff; the listeners were left running.
- The filesystem is nearly full after the host rebuild and package stage; only
  about 350 MiB was free after `ORBW00020` packaging. Avoid further duplicate
  packages or build trees until space is recovered. Invalid
  `ORBW00007/08` local package directories were removed to make space;
  their results are documented. Preserve the working `ORBW00011` package
  stage as the A/B base. All current working A/B packages and their stages
  remain in `pkgout/orbis-*`.

### Oblivion input compared with Pinball (2026-10-14)

Pinball is the known-good controller-input baseline: its profile maps the
DualSense to keyboard keys and it does not use DirectInput. Oblivion does use
DirectInput 8. Runtime logs show `NtUserRegisterRawInputDevices` with flags
`0x230` (DirectInput's exclusive keyboard/mouse registration), and mapped
controller keys arrive as `WM_INPUT` on DirectInput's hidden window. Because
exclusive DirectInput requests `RIDEV_NOLEGACY`, Wine suppresses the ordinary
`WM_KEYDOWN`/`WM_KEYUP` route. Thus Pinball and Oblivion exercise different
input paths even with `[input] mode = keyboard`; a key remap alone cannot
establish that Oblivion receives the input as intended.

The most recent Oblivion log is `build-ps4-logs/ps5log-20261014-172729.log`.
L1/Enter down and up reached `NtUserSendHardwareInput` for the game window
with status 0; its WM_INPUT wake was dequeued by the DirectInput thread. Cross
mouse input likewise produced WM_INPUT. This establishes queue acceptance and
raw-input delivery, but does not prove the game consumed the event or explain
the observed slowdown. Pinball's success isolates the difference to the
title's DirectInput path, not the physical controller. The user chose to test
keyboard/mouse emulation against Oblivion's PC defaults, rather than depend on
a virtual DirectInput gamepad. `src/profiles/oblivion-gog.profile` now maps
Cross=Space (activate), Circle=Tab (journal), Square=F (ready/sheathe),
Triangle=E (jump), L1=Alt (block), R1=mouse-left (attack), L2=Z (grab),
R2=C (cast), L3=Ctrl (sneak), R3=R (change view), Options=Escape, Create=T
(wait), D-pad=arrows, and right stick=mouse. Oblivion's `bUse Joystick` stays
disabled. This profile was uploaded to
`/data/prospero-win/profiles/oblivion-gog.profile` and verified byte-for-byte
(SHA256 `4e743c1e92767f5d107183f317ed7d876f7c94e03760a5667142b9885ceb41f2`).
No package rebuild is needed. The current mapper has no left-stick-to-WASD
binding, so this tests button actions and menus rather than complete PS3-style
movement. If the button slowdown persists, add targeted `+dinput` logging to
confirm the raw-input-to-DirectInput state update; keep the stalled four-frame
render loop separate unless a trace links it to input handling.

The user reports the remap still lags and the buttons do not work, matching the
earlier MDK symptom. I added `+dinput` to the profile's existing
`+ps5drv,+msg` trace and uploaded it to the same path. The remote profile was
downloaded and byte-compared; SHA256 is
`4fb78bbc979f5c9804ad3f98fda241711707a61c8e8261c1ac32f5d68da1475c`.

The subsequent capture resolves part of the input path. Cross reaches DirectInput
as Space (`scan 0x39`, down and up), and R1 reaches it as a left mouse-button
event. The game did not respond to those actions at the new-game confirmation
prompt. An earlier D-pad Down trace reached scan `0x50` (keypad 2), motivating
the extended-key fix; the corrected package still needs a D-pad test.

The next Oblivion run used the quieter `+ps5drv` profile and Cross=Enter. The
trace confirms the PS4 pad edge was detected and Enter down/up reached
`NtUserSendHardwareInput` successfully. However, the game stayed at four
rendered frames from 19:50:24 onward, including before the Enter presses, and
the menu did not activate. This keeps the title's render/input stall separate
from whether the physical button is read. The current console profile maps
Cross to `mouse_left` for one more confirmation-prompt test; no package rebuild
is needed, but Oblivion must be relaunched to load it.

To stop testing through a stalled title, a standalone controller-map app was
added and uploaded as `GLDS10048` (`/data/pkg/controller-map.pkg`). It displays
live PS4 controller states alongside their keyboard/mouse assignments and has
an in-memory edit mode (Options toggles it; D-pad selects; Left/Right changes
the action). It runs the PS4 pad adapter without Wine, isolating pad reads and
mapping from Wine and the game. Changes are logged to klog but are not yet
saved into the Oblivion profile. Source and usage are in
`src/controller-map/README.md`; package SHA256 is
`631475eca8ab0055ad812ed5a4708e8f8f35fa6c60fe7e0e8a31a2c65bc15d23`.
The user verified the normal mapping and edit controls on-console. Touchpad is
not represented; Oblivion does not bind it, so this is an accepted limitation
for this test.

The PE32 Wine viewer's initial build and upload hash was
`f080c6c926bf6dd67fbbbfb9fcb485cb5bd305d3081b5fe94509e53a0d5b0dbf`. It shows
DirectInput's current state for all keys in the Oblivion mapping, Space/Enter/

lets the next run test whether the injected mouse/key reaches DirectInput
without requiring Oblivion to respond.

The controller-map run confirms the PS4 reads the DualShock 4 and the on-screen
edit path responds; the touchpad remains out of scope. To isolate the remaining

`/data/prospero-win/prefixes/oblivion-gog/drive_c/Games/OblivionGOG/input-viewer.exe`.
The new `oblivion-input-test` profile is in

so the previous CPU/sink counter interpretation did not establish the cause of

### Bounded DirectInput delivery trace (2026-10-14)

Do not repeat an Oblivion launch with the same package/profile: the user is
right that it would add no evidence. The successful PE64 DirectInput viewer
already demonstrates key transitions reaching DirectInput in that process;
Oblivion is PE32, uses the WoW64 path, and the game itself remains unresponsive.
GTA IV uses the profile's XInput path, so its success does not validate the
DirectInput route.

I added `prospero-win/wine/patches/0864-dinput-trace-ps4-key-delivery.patch`.
It adds low-volume WARN records at DirectInput's keyboard event handler,
buffered-event return, and non-empty `GetDeviceState` polls (rate-limited to
once per 250 ms). This distinguishes an event that reaches DirectInput from
one the game polls as state, without enabling the high-volume `+dinput` and
`+msg` channels that confounded the earlier latency test.

The first full host Wine link exposed that patch `0611` used `debugstr_w` in
`wow64.dll`, pulling in an unresolved `IsBadStringPtrW` import. I changed only
those diagnostic messages to report the selected backend index, preserving
the CPU selection behavior while avoiding the wide-string formatter. The
patch series check then passed, and the full clean host build and install
completed in `~/codex_scratch/wine-host` (`wine-11.17-54-g490f6d5dcb`, 1.8 GB
installed). Both installed DirectInput DLLs contain the expected
`PS4-DI event`, `PS4-DI queue`, and `PS4-DI poll` trace strings.

I refreshed the test package from the existing relocatable `GLDS10056` stage,
replacing only i386 and x86_64 `dinput.dll`. A recursive comparison confirms
those are the only payload differences; all 1,782 staged payload hashes
passed. The local package is
`pkgout/input-directinput-trace/IV0000-GLDS10056_00-GLDS100560000000.pkg`,
SHA256 `f957ad9b0d1d31cd0f6598904bb00f98a8994ff3ec87df05073bf7a57a9ee018`.
The first upload attempt, while the console was unreachable, failed with
`No route to host`. After the user brought the PS4 back online on 2026-10-15,
the package uploaded successfully to `/data/pkg/wine-input-fix-test.pkg`
(GoldHEN FTP returned `226`). SHA256 remains
`f957ad9b0d1d31cd0f6598904bb00f98a8994ff3ec87df05073bf7a57a9ee018`.

### 2026-10-15: latest Oblivion run and Raw Input follow-up

The user reports the latest run still has the same whole-screen hitch on input and
cannot enter the game. The Options press recovered, but did not advance past the
start-new-game prompt. This remains reproducible; the diagnostic package has not
fixed it.

The 06:23 run in `build-ps4-logs/ps5log-20261015-055326.log` confirms the
PS4 mapping sends Options as Escape (`vk=0x1b`, scan `0x1`) down and up. The app
registers Raw Input and Wine emits `fixme:rawinput:NtUserRegisterRawInputDevices
Unhandled flags 0x230`. Wine's implementation handles the `RIDEV_NOLEGACY`
portion (`0x30`) but does not implement the other `0x200` flag. We still do not
know whether Oblivion receives and reads the resulting `WM_INPUT` events. The
same run stayed alive with audio advancing and only four frames submitted; do not
use those counters as a frame-rate measurement.

To close that evidence gap without another package build, I changed
`src/profiles/oblivion-gog.profile` to set
`winedebug = +ps5drv,warn+dinput,trace+rawinput` and uploaded it to
`/data/prospero-win/profiles/oblivion-gog.profile` (FTP `226`). The next launch
should show `NtUserRegisterRawInputDevices` and `NtUserGetRawInputData` traces.
A single Options press is sufficient: registration plus GetRawInputData calls
will tell us whether the game's input thread consumes Raw Input. If no read is
logged, next instrument the server's `queue_rawinput_message`/message retrieval
route or test whether its `RIDEV_NOLEGACY` registration suppresses the injected
keyboard path. If reads do occur, compare timestamps with the visual hitch and
trace the game's event handler or message-pump path. Avoid another generic
DirectInput test; it already showed that the controller map itself works.

accepts every event, nor explain the hitch.

does not include HRESULT, requested count, `DIGDD_PEEK`, acquire/cooperative
level, caller address, or elapsed time. Also, `GetDeviceState` reports a held
key only while the key is down, with a 250 ms rate limit. The next useful
diagnostic should add those fields around DirectInput Acquire,
SetCooperativeLevel, GetDeviceState, and GetDeviceData, recording timestamps
and thread IDs. That can distinguish intermittent acquisition/queue behavior
from a game-side menu issue and show whether any DirectInput call itself
accounts for the visible stall. Avoid another controller test or remap-only
package: the existing run already demonstrates the mapped down/up path.

### 2026-10-15: slow DirectInput poll narrowed to event-pump boundary

The 08:14 run of the first status package logged one
`GetDeviceData` call taking 30 ms with an empty keyboard queue (`request=1`,
`returned=0`, `hr=DI_OK`, thread 36). It did not coincide with a logged pad
transition. `GetDeviceData` invokes `IDirectInputDevice2_Poll`; the keyboard
poll calls `check_dinput_events()`, whose implementation calls
`MsgWaitForMultipleObjectsEx(0, NULL, 0, QS_ALLINPUT, 0)`. This makes the
zero-timeout message pump a concrete candidate for that stall, but the single
record does not prove it was the slow portion.

I added `prospero-win/wine/patches/0869-dinput-trace-ps4-event-pump-latency.patch`
to time that exact call and emit only if it takes at least 5 ms, rate-limited
to once per 250 ms. The previous build also logged repeated already-acquired
`Acquire` results every ~17 ms; patch 0868 now limits those no-op records to
once per second. Both 32-bit and 64-bit DINPUT8 DLLs compile with both changes.
The package is
`pkgout/input-directinput8-pump/IV0000-GLDS10056_00-GLDS100560000000.pkg`,
SHA256 `99428ffbe975155bc93b896bffad005e0fe10b3646c402b2b8cbe807a6f2ae99`.
Both architectures' staged DLLs contain the event-pump and slow-read markers;
the patch reverse-checks against the built source. It now replaces
`/data/pkg/wine-input-fix-test.pkg` (FTP `226`). The active run still has the
earlier package loaded. On the next launch, compare any `PS4-DI slow event
pump` record with `PS4-DI slow GetDeviceData`: matching durations would localize
the delay to the message pump; no slow pump record would move the investigation
to the rest of `keyboard_poll()` / input processing or other game threads.

status remains the known missing-PS5-native-tool behavior, so validate its

placed under repo-local `codex_scratch/tmp` because `/tmp` was full.

### 2026-10-15: zero-timeout queue spin confirmed

The 0906 run confirms the input-stall path. Wine logged the keyboard input
events, and the runtime counter rose to 267 input events. A slow zero-timeout
`wait_message()` call spent 1,034 ms in `NtWaitForMultipleObjects`, repeated
12,657 times, while `process_driver_events()` took 0 ms. Other samples spent
300–900 ms there with thousands of queue retries. This explains why clicks can
arrive but freeze the visible game: the poll re-waits on a signaled server
queue even after driver processing says there is no matching input. Surface
flush remains 0–1 ms, so graphics flushing is not the cause of this stall.

### 2026-10-15: native WoW64 signal probe and opt-in candidate

The first PS4 v0.2.0 native-backend launch failed. The exact runtime refusal is
not present in the current local klog, so do not treat the following as a
captured line from that run: the current source explains the expected failure
path. `wow64native` calls `__wine_prospero_native_wow64_caps(1)` and returns
`STATUS_NOT_SUPPORTED` unless all three capability bits are set. The PS4
source still routes readiness through the PS5 libkernel resolver, which leaves
its raw signal/sigreturn/sysarch entries unavailable on Orbis; therefore ntdll
normally returns zero and Wine's new fallback selects `wowprospero.dll`.

I built and uploaded side-by-side probe ORBW00007
(`ORBW00007.pkg` in `/data/pkg`). It enters compatibility CS `0x33`, sets a
low guest FS marker, reads `FS:0`, delivers two `int3` signals on an alternate
stack, and checks handler entry/return, CS, `mc_len`, saved guest FS, the
marker, XMM0 in the signal frame, and repeated XMM/YMM restoration. `RESULT
PASS` also requires AVX and XCR0 `0x7`, matching the native backend's current
xstate gate. This validates ordinary kernel signal-frame layout and sigreturn;
it does not exercise Wine's exception dispatcher or context-edit/restore path.
Its source and builder are in
`~/codex_scratch/prospero-win-orbis-upstream/tools/`. The probe has not yet
been run on the console; run it before installing the native candidate.

In `~/codex_scratch/prospero-win-orbis-upstream`, patch
`wine/orbis/base-patches/0909-ntdll-orbis-native-wow64-signal-provider.patch`
adds an Orbis signal-provider path behind compile-time
`PW_ORBIS_NATIVE_WOW64_TEST=1`. The default remains closed. In this explicit
test build, native readiness is reported only if Wine's Orbis signal handlers
registered, Orbis `sysarch` is available, CS/DS are `0x43`/`0x3b`, the enabled
xstate mask is exactly `0x7`, and the AVX component matches the PS5 layout
(offset 576, size 256, with at least 320 bytes of signal xstate storage). Orbis
uses libc `sigaction` and kernel signal return rather than the PS5 raw signal
entries. These runtime checks validate registration and metadata, not actual
compat-mode delivery/return; ORBW00007 remains the required hardware check.
The patch reverse-applies cleanly, and both opt-in and default
compiler-wrapper behavior were checked.

ORBW00008 (SHA-256
`4c15be47b75020fd751ac37c09b157da59c963dfc11e7e3d4465a2cc41db091e`) is
superseded because its readiness gate skipped the xstate checks. The corrected
PRXs were rebuilt and packaged as ORBW00025 at
`pkgout/orbis-native-wow64-relocatable-025/IV0000-ORBW00025_00-ORBW000250000000.pkg`
with the relocatable handoff: a low-address stub `eboot.bin` loads the
high-address `sce_module/relocated_wine_app.prx`, whose main releases the eboot's
conflicting low segments immediately before launching the selected Windows
process. This preserves Oblivion's non-relocatable PE32 image at `0x400000`.
Package SHA-256 is
`37f93231e0aa8833d305e22dcf8111964c4374702aea08bce6711c65c70e85a5`; eboot
SHA-256 is `835903778d52311d93b60376fc067202c9df5b541c7a7d4da7bbfc2b836363c8`
and relocated app PRX SHA-256 is
`c7653ffc0835eac957d43659311c1be1ff1ef8296f9cfe831caacd4bdd5706e1`. Both
images were checked as DYN before packaging, and both ntdll copies match the
rebuilt signal-gated module. The corrected package replaced the earlier

The klog receiver is already connected to port 3232, and the current UDP

Hardware runs 2026-10-15: both ORBW00007 attempts failed before the probe's
first log line, so neither produced signal results. The first package's ELF
had VMA 0; the console mapped it at `0x400000`, but the absolute pointer in
`.data.sce_process_param` remained `0x87548`. Klog reported
`copyin=0xe uaddr=0x87548` and fatal `0xa0020105 (PRX_INVALID_IMAGE)` in
`libkernel.sprx+0x28862`.

The next ELF was linked at VMA `0x400000`. Klog then showed the title mapped at
`0x800000` (text `0x800000..0x884000`, data `0x890000..0x904000`), while the
process-parameter pointers still held `0x487488`, `0x487518`, and `0x487548`.
The kernel again faulted at the same `libkernel` instruction, now with
`copyin=0xe uaddr=0x487548`. The `0x400000` discrepancy between linked and
runtime addresses identifies the load bias: absolute pointers in the process
parameter block need the same bias as the image segments. Linking at the
console's runtime base is not sufficient by itself.

The third package added `0x400000` to those three pointers, making them
`0x887488`, `0x887518`, and `0x887548`, and was uploaded to the same path
(FTP 226; SHA-256
`3bf52eef53fe5735a5fda3df2efbfea572333cbc05f502dce445b7c4707f8d1c`). It
passed the process-parameter read, but the 19:07:49 run faulted at
`0x0c5a0e8`. Klog shows the branch came from `libkernel.sprx+0x28851`; the
target is the ELF entry `0x45a0e8` plus an 8 MiB bias, outside the mapped
EBOOT at `0x800000`. Thus linking at VMA `0x400000` also double-biases the
entry point. No probe code or signal test ran.

The next build restores ET_EXEC VMA 0 and entry `0x5a0e8`, which the console
maps at `0x400000`, and adds only `0x400000` to the three process-parameter
pointers. They now read `0x487488`, `0x487518`, and `0x487548`, matching the
runtime addresses in that image. This package was uploaded to
`/data/pkg/IV0000-ORBW00007_00-ORBW000070000000.pkg` (FTP 226; SHA-256
`f84c46ff88f1c5d50d02c07ce7b58018e04130141aaa1cfd82f0020b1690e5b9`). The
19:12:20 run proved the process parameters and entry point are now accepted:
the probe printed its first line. It then faulted inside `libkernel.sprx` with

unsupported.

## 2026-10-15 20:37 — guest-FS compat signal pass and real thread-data follow-up

The `guestfs-synthetic-tcb` package completed on hardware and remained on its

```text
RESULT signals=3 unexpected=0 unexpected_rip=0 signals_seen=5,5,5 avx=1 xcr0=7 guest_tls_base=498600 compat_eax=51a7c0de cs=43,33,33 mc_len=1152,1152,1152 fsbase=880dac5e0,498600,498600 xstate_bv=1,7,7 rip=8000015bc,4000ed,4000ee
RESULT xstate xmm=0,1,1 ymm_hi=0,1,1 returned_vector=1
RESULT diagnostic signal returns count=3 cs=43,33,33
```

This establishes that low guest FS works during compatibility-mode execution,
that compat `int3` reaches the registered SIGTRAP handler and returns normally
when guest `FS:0x10` points to mapped writable memory, and that the signal
frames retain XMM/YMM state. It does not yet establish that the scratch block
has the correct Orbis thread-data layout or is suitable for Wine.

Correction to the earlier disassembly notes above: the exact libkernel dump is
`codex_scratch/libkernel-ps4.sprx` (converted to
`codex_scratch/libkernel-ps4-inspect.elf`; extracted executable PT_LOAD is
`codex_scratch/libkernel-text-full.bin`). The signal dispatcher reads
`FS:0x10` at module offset `+0xc929`, passes that pointer to `+0x4a30`, and
then calls the signal handler. `+0x4a30` writes `dword [ptr+0xd8]=1` and
checks fields at `+0xd4`, `+0xd0`, `+0xdc`, `+0x2c`, and `+0x30`; the return
path calls `+0x4ae0`, which clears `[ptr+0xd8]`. The helper beginning at
`+0x2c70` reads `FS:0x10` at `+0x2ca9` and can return `ptr+0x188`. The former
statement that the dispatcher read FS at `+0xc72a` (or that the write was
`+0xc774`) was incorrect; those offsets must not be used. The existing
`+0x188` fault attribution matches the helper's pointer arithmetic, but the
faulting path and role of that helper are not yet fully traced.

A next package was built to replace the synthetic scratch pointer with the
actual qword read from native host `FS:0x10`, and to log the six libkernel-
examined fields before and after the two compat traps. This directly tests
whether the PS4 thread's real libkernel metadata is usable from guest FS. It
also addresses the production constraint in
`$SCRATCH_ROOT/prospero-win-orbis-upstream/wine/wow64native/README.md`:
Wine explicitly warns against storing host TLS pointers in the guest TEB's
`FiberData` or arbitrary-user-pointer fields because those fields are used by
fibers and DLL loading. Wine's x86 TEB uses offset `0x10` for `NT_TIB.FiberData`,
so the synthetic successful test is a hardware primitive, not an acceptable
production fix by itself.

`pkgout/orbis-native32-signal-probe-real-tcb/IV0000-ORBW00007_00-ORBW000070000000.pkg`,

### 2026-10-15 20:47 — actual libkernel thread-data pointer passed through guest FS

The `real-tcb` package launched at 20:47:39 and completed without an
unexpected signal. The decisive results were:

```text
RESULT signals=3 unexpected=0 unexpected_rip=0 signals_seen=5,5,5 avx=1 xcr0=7 guest_tls_base=4985e0 compat_eax=51a7c0de cs=43,33,33 mc_len=1152,1152,1152 fsbase=880dac5e0,4985e0,4985e0 xstate_bv=1,7,7 rip=8000015bc,405ded,405dee
RESULT xstate xmm=0,1,1 ymm_hi=0,1,1 returned_vector=1
RESULT diagnostic signal returns count=3 cs=43,33,33
RESULT real-tcb ptr=880f4c540 offsets=d0,d4,d8,dc,2c,30 before=1,0,0,0,0,0 after=1,0,0,0,0,0
```

Unlike the prior synthetic-pointer run, guest `FS:0x10` contained the actual
qword read from native host `FS:0x10` (`0x880f4c540`). Both compat traps were
delivered and returned. The libkernel thread-data busy flag at `+0xd8` was
zero before and after; the other inspected fields were unchanged. This
confirms the real Orbis thread-data object remains usable when its pointer is
supplied through guest FS during compat signal delivery.

This still does not directly validate Wine's guest TEB. A 64-bit libkernel
load of `FS:0x10` spans the 32-bit TEB's `NT_TIB.FiberData` at `+0x10` and
`ArbitraryUserPointer` at `+0x14`; storing the real pointer there would consume
both Windows fields. Wine uses `FiberData` for fiber conversion/switching, and
other code may use `ArbitraryUserPointer`. The synthetic TCB result is only a
signal-path experiment, not an acceptable generic TEB change. The next code
review must identify a way for Orbis signal entry to access its per-thread
metadata while preserving those Windows-visible fields, or establish a
supported Wine-side virtualization for them. Keep the native capability gate
closed until that design also passes Wine exception, context-edit, thread, and
fiber tests.

The current `real-tcb` test package remains uploaded at
`/data/pkg/IV0000-ORBW00007_00-ORBW000070000000.pkg`; its local artifact and
SHA-256 are recorded above. Klog continues on TCP 3232 in
`build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261015-193628.log`.

### Next probe — Sony exception callback with guest `FS:0x10` cleared

The POSIX `sigaction` route is proven with both synthetic memory and the actual
Orbis thread-data pointer. The Sony SIGTRAP callback experiment did not reach
the callback: its log ended at `RESULT FAIL Sony SIGTRAP registration`. That
package is not evidence about the guest TCB or callback delivery; Sony refused
registration for SIGTRAP.

The replacement probe keeps ordinary POSIX SIGTRAP for the native baseline,
then registers only Sony SIGSEGV, clears the POSIX SIGSEGV action, and faults
on address 1 in compat mode while guest `FS:0x10` is zero. Its callback entry
restores host FS before recording the saved frame and redirects saved RIP to
the compat-to-native return sequence. This tests whether Sony's exception
callback can bypass the normal libkernel signal trampoline's guest-TCB lookup.

toolchain; the ELF has 0x4000-aligned PT_LOADs and the required
`_start`/`_sceProcessParam` symbols. Package:
`pkgout/orbis-native32-signal-probe-sony-sigsegv/IV0000-ORBW00007_00-ORBW000070000000.pkg`,

It has replaced `/data/pkg/IV0000-ORBW00007_00-ORBW000070000000.pkg` via FTP

`build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261015-193628.log`.

`sceKernelInstallExceptionHandler(SIGSEGV)` returned `0`. The fault then
crashed inside libkernel at `base+0xc776`, with fault address `0x2c` and
`r13=0`. Disassembly of the captured PS4 libkernel shows the sequence reads
`FS:0x10` into `r13` at `+0xc72a`, then dereferences `[r13+0x2c]` at `+0xc776`.
The Sony callback therefore did not bypass the libkernel trampoline; with a
null guest `FS:0x10`, the trampoline crashes before invoking the registered
callback. This is direct evidence that simply switching registration APIs
cannot remove the guest-TEB collision.

The next probe keeps Sony SIGSEGV registration but copies the actual pointer
from host `FS:0x10` into the probe's low guest-TLS `FS:0x10` slot before the
compat fault. This isolates whether the callback and edited-context return
work once the trampoline's required TCB pointer is present. It is diagnostic
only; it does not make repurposing Windows `FiberData`/`ArbitraryUserPointer`
acceptable for Wine.

The follow-up package is
`pkgout/orbis-native32-signal-probe-sony-real-tcb/IV0000-ORBW00007_00-ORBW000070000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `1b6cd871e652adce72eea12c0ad480b4d5c3ff300671bfd01a164bcbb997cb5f`.
It replaced the standard FTP package path successfully (226). Install and
launch it for the next test. Klog remains connected at
`build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261015-193628.log`.

The user launched it at 21:03:39. The app completed the Sony SIGSEGV callback
round trip:

```text
stage copied host signal TCB=880f4c540 to guest FS:0x10
stage Sony SIGSEGV registration rc=0/0x0
RESULT signals=2 unexpected=11 unexpected_rip=405df1 signals_seen=5,11,0 avx=1 xcr0=7 guest_tls_base=4985e0 compat_eax=51a7c0de cs=43,33,0 mc_len=1152,1152,0 fsbase=880dac5e0,4985e0,0 xstate_bv=3,7,0 rip=8000015bc,405df1,0
RESULT xstate xmm=0,1,0 ymm_hi=0,1,0 returned_vector=1
```

`unexpected=11` is the probe's expected SIGSEGV marker: its C handler rewrites
the saved compat RIP to the far-return stub, which returned to 64-bit code and
printed the result. The previous null-slot run crashed in libkernel before the
callback; with the real pointer, registration, compat fault delivery, handler
entry, edited-context return, and vector preservation all succeeded. This
confirms the callback API is usable, but also confirms the callback does not
bypass libkernel's requirement that guest `FS:0x10` point to the host TCB.

Do not enable native WoW64 yet. Static review confirms the collision is real:
Wine's `TEB32` starts with `NT_TIB32`, whose `FiberData` is at `+0x10` and
`ArbitraryUserPointer` at `+0x14` (`include/winnt.h`). `virtual.c:init_teb()`
initializes the fiber field, `GetCurrentFiber()` / `GetFiberData()` read it,
and `ntdll/loader.c` temporarily writes `ArbitraryUserPointer` while processing
DLL notifications. The qword libkernel consumes therefore overlaps two live
Windows ABI fields. The probe's low TLS buffer is not a Wine TEB. A PS4-only
mirror/alias would need coherent updates for the full 4-KiB `TEB32` page and
all cross-bitness Wine accesses; simply storing the host pointer in either
field is not a valid generic fix. Keep the native capability gate closed and
translator fallback enabled unless a TEB virtualization design is implemented
and tested for fibers, DLL loading, exceptions, and multiple threads.

Wine registers SIGTRAP during startup, but hardware showed Sony refuses
`sceKernelInstallExceptionHandler(SIGTRAP)`. The package therefore leaves
SIGTRAP on libc's handler and routes only SIGSEGV/SIGBUS/SIGILL/SIGFPE through

published.

This is intentionally an assumption test, not a safe/general native backend.
It does not virtualize the TEB32 `FiberData`/`ArbitraryUserPointer` overlap at
guest `FS:0x10`; the probe only established that the Sony callback succeeds
when that slot contains the host TCB. If ORBW00026 enters native execution, a
signal may still expose this known collision. Watch klog for
`PW_NATIVE_PROVIDER caps=...` and the `wow64native` initialization/fallback
status. A fallback means the handler registration or a hardware readiness
check did not satisfy the gate. A crash at signal delivery is evidence about
the unresolved TEB/host-FS bridge; it does not invalidate the tested PS4
signal frame or Sony callback primitive.

### ORBW00029 repeat run and coredump capture (2026-10-16)

The user relaunched ORBW00029 and reproduced the same SIGSYS at
`0x80000028bc` (klog 08:03:26.878, PID 117). The one-shot watcher successfully
retrieved the coredump before Crash Reporter cleanup:
`build-ps4-logs/ORBW00029_1792163006-080327.sorbisdmp`, 145,472 bytes,
SHA-256 `bd73c6656cad4ca927e2616d88e5d49a470d2a547f4f4ce21abe7c4245545f06`.
Its first eight bytes are `00 00 46 41 43 45 43 53` (the little-endian
`SCECAF` signature). It is not a raw ELF or a gzip stream, and standard gzip
and zlib decoders do not decode it; its payload appears containerized and
requires a CAF-aware extractor before the thread context can be read. The
repeat run's register state and backtrace are identical to the previous SIGSYS.
The dynamic-library table maps the fault to `libkernel.sprx+0x28bc`, and maps
the saved return addresses through ntdll, the relocated Wine app, and
`libkernel.sprx+0x75c2`. `addr2line` on the matching `app.shared.elf` resolves
the relocated-app frames to `parse_sections` and `pw_wine_launch_parse`.
Those frames identify the surrounding call path but do not identify the
faulting libkernel function. The run still fails before native WoW64 guest
execution; resolve the libkernel syscall/frame first, then choose a targeted
instrumentation change instead of another assumption package.

The PS4 syscall reference says an unimplemented syscall is handled by
`nosys`, which returns `ENOSYS` and sends `SIGSYS` to the caller
(https://www.psdevwiki.com/ps4/Syscalls). That makes an unsupported syscall a
plausible explanation for the signal, but not a proven one: this klog does not
include `si_code` or a syscall number. The next diagnostic should obtain the
SIGSYS-specific `siginfo` fields from the PS4 ABI, or resolve
`libkernel.sprx+0x28bc` against the exact firmware's symbols. Avoid another
same-build launch; it has reproduced the same state several times.

### Upstream comparison: DirectInput stall

The upstream `prospero-win` v0.2.0 build notes document the same class of
controller-induced stall: its PS5 driver's `ProcessEvents` consumed a shared
wake pipe but reported no event handled, leaving `QS_DRIVER` set and the
zero-timeout DirectInput message wait spinning until new input. Upstream's
patch 0879 fixes the wake handling. Our PS4 measurements found the same
zero-timeout queue spin (up to 12,657 retries in `NtWaitForMultipleObjects`),
and PS4 patch 0907 bounds the unsuccessful zero-timeout retry; patch 0903
ports the consumed-wakeup-as-handled behavior. The PS5-side implementation
provides strong upstream precedent, while the exact PS4 user-driver and wake
plumbing remains platform-specific. Upstream also documents patch 0470, its
DualSense-to-XInput controller bridge; the console-specific pad reader and
runtime sink still require a PS4 port.

`build-ps4-logs/ps5log-20261015-091636.log` as they append.

### ORBW00030 PS4 native signal bridge attempt 033 (2026-10-16)

The diagnostic DLL in package 032 showed why native WoW64 stopped before the
game: `BTCpuProcessInit` reached the PS4 provider, but `PW_NATIVE_PROVIDER`
advertised `caps=0 raw=0 return=0 sysarch=0` and `pw_native_process_init`
returned `STATUS_NOT_SUPPORTED` (`c00000bb`). The PS5 resolver intentionally
requires raw libkernel signal entry points that do not exist on PS4. The PS4
run had already established ordinary `sigaction` delivery, and the selector
and xstate checks passed, so this was a provider gate mismatch rather than an
Oblivion loader failure.

Added `src/wine-patches-ps4/0910-ntdll-ps4-native-wow64-signal-bridge.patch`.
On PS4 it routes registered signals through the existing host-FS-restoring
entries and only advertises native capability after `sysarch(AMD64_GET_FSBASE)`
returns a nonzero host base; the existing selector/xstate checks remain, and
the PS5 raw-entry gate is unchanged. The patch series checker now permits
0910. This is a hardware experiment, not proof that the whole native backend
is safe: the known guest TEB32 `FS:0x10` collision remains unresolved and could
surface after startup.

Rebuilt the Wine core (`ntdll.so: built=True`, no unresolved ntdll imports),
then relinked PS4 PRXs, RADV, FreeType, and wineps5. Built relocatable package
033 with the diagnostic `wow64native.dll`, retaining title ID ORBW00030:
`pkgout/orbis-native-companion-033/IV0000-ORBW00030_00-ORBW000300000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `191ed6abc4d48b16ae8b7e08004e2603db51db12d496abb13bd6fc77196c6470`.
Uploaded to `/data/pkg/libkernelprobe.pkg`; GoldHEN returned `226`. Install this
update before the next Oblivion launch. Do not stop or restart the listeners.

### ORBW00030 package 033: later native-start crash identified (2026-10-16)

The 033 run passed the previous startup gate: `PW_NATIVE_PROVIDER caps=7`,
`wow64native initialized`, and `BTCpuProcessInit status=00000000`. Wine then
loaded the native Oblivion image at `0x00400000` and continued initializing
system DLLs. It ran about 18 seconds from title launch before a host SIGSEGV;
package 032 had exited about 8 seconds after launch with
`BTCpuProcessInit status=c00000bb`. This is real progress in diagnosis, but
not a stable native-mode startup.

The new klog fault is thread `orbis`, signal 11, read fault at `0x1f88`, with
RIP `libkernel.sprx+0xc742`. Disassembly of the matching saved PS4 libkernel
shows this is in its signal-delivery path: the code calls the helper at
`+0x2c70`, then dereferences the returned pointer. At this fault, `r14=10`
(the signal being processed), and the helper returns `FS:0x10 + 0x188`; the
observed return/fault address `0x1f88` therefore implies guest `FS:0x10` held
`0x1e00`. That value is a 32-bit Wine TEB field pair, not the Orbis thread-data
pointer. This matches the earlier probe findings: libkernel's normal signal
trampoline consumes `FS:0x10` as its thread-data pointer, while the x86 TEB
uses the same bytes for `FiberData` and `ArbitraryUserPointer`.

The 0910 signal-bridge readiness gate allowed native mode to start but did not
solve that per-thread FS/TEB collision. Do not interpret this as evidence that
the CPU backend itself is failing, nor keep launching the game with this gate
open. The next engineering step is a proper per-thread signal/TLS bridge that
preserves the Windows TEB fields, or to restore translator fallback until
that bridge exists. The log also reports `wgl:internal_context_create Failed
to create internal thread context` and no frames were presented; that may be a
separate graphics/thread issue, but the fatal signal trampoline fault is the
first concrete crash cause to address.

The 033 runtime log is `build-ps4-logs/ps5log-20261015-091636.log`; the exact
fault and register dump are in `build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261016-063744.log`
around 09:51:32. The console cleaned up its coredump within seconds, so no
dump was retrieved. Keep both existing listeners running.

### ORBW00031: test for a separate kernel signal TLS base (2026-10-16)

Research found a newer FreeBSD design that directly addresses this class of
fault: `AMD64_SET_TLSBASE` stores a separate per-thread TLS base that the
kernel installs in FS on signal-handler entry, even while compat code uses FS
segmentation. It is a FreeBSD 15-era addition, so support on the PS4 firmware
is unknown and must be measured rather than assumed. See FreeBSD's
[PCB_TLSBASE change](https://reviews.freebsd.org/D50482) and its
[amd64 sysarch operation definitions](https://github.com/freebsd/freebsd-src/blob/main/sys/x86/include/sysarch.h).

Built side-by-side package ORBW00031, “Signal TLSBASE Probe.” It calls
`sysarch(AMD64_SET_TLSBASE=136, &host_fs)` before the compatibility-mode fault.
If the operation is rejected, it logs `TLSBASE_UNSUPPORTED` and waits without
attempting the fault. If accepted, it no longer overwrites guest `FS:0x10`;
it tries the existing compat SIGSEGV callback and records the signal frame.
This tests both firmware support and whether that kernel mechanism fixes the
libkernel trampoline collision. Source/build: `codex_scratch/orbw00031-tlsbase/`
and `build/orbw00031-tlsbase/`. Package:
`pkgout/orbw00031-tlsbase/IV0000-ORBW00031_00-ORBW000310000000.pkg`, SHA-256
`df2e6a9e804e81f8e66fa9e723408d7b68122cd1e5b1b5f2eb012f392bd41f3e`.
Uploaded via the no-login FTP writer to `/data/pkg/orbw00031-tlsbase.pkg`
(226). The first package was installed and run, but crashed before `main()`:
its GP4 had an empty `sce_module/`, producing `0xa0020102`
(`PRX_SCE_MODULE_LOAD_ERROR`). The failure was packaging, not evidence about
`AMD64_SET_TLSBASE`.

Rebuilt and replaced it at the same FTP path with `libc.prx` and
`libSceFios2.prx` included under `sce_module/`. Corrected package:
`pkgout/orbw00031-tlsbase-fixed/IV0000-ORBW00031_00-ORBW000310000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `b78e2f4aac42fe3f0b3558f1fa4094fb9305b11c48b69ef70d933479c549aace`
(FTP 226). Install this corrected package and rerun the probe. The 033 wine
package remains an unsafe native-mode experiment; keep translator fallback as
the default until the signal path is made safe.

### ORBW00031 corrected package: PS4 rejects separate TLSBASE (2026-10-16)

The corrected package was installed and launched successfully. Klog confirms
`main()` ran and reported:

```text
stage get-host-fs ok
stage AMD64_SET_TLSBASE rc=-1 errno=22 base=880dac5e0
RESULT TLSBASE_UNSUPPORTED; compat fault not attempted
```

The corrected package did not crash; it remains in its intentional wait loop.
This firmware rejects sysarch operation 136 (`AMD64_SET_TLSBASE`) with
`EINVAL`, so the FreeBSD separate signal-TLS-base facility is unavailable on
this PS4. Do not repeat this probe or infer anything about compat signal return
from this run. The prior synthetic and real-TCB probes already establish that
compat signal delivery works when `FS:0x10` points to a writable signal TCB;
the unresolved problem is preserving the two Windows TEB32 fields at that
location. Next work should review a direct kernel signal-registration/return
path or design a Wine-side TEB bridge, while keeping native WoW64 fail-closed
for ordinary games.

### ORBW00032: preserve the non-fiber TEB sentinel while satisfying signal entry (2026-10-16)

Because PS4 rejects `AMD64_SET_TLSBASE`, the next narrow test asks whether the
existing non-fiber `TEB32` values can remain intact while libkernel gets a
writable signal TCB. The crash register/log evidence showed Wine's
`FiberData=0x1e00` and `ArbitraryUserPointer=0`; the probe maps one page at
`0x1000`, places a synthetic TCB at `0x1e00`, and sets guest `FS:0x10` to that
same qword. It logs and waits safely if the fixed low mapping is rejected. If
mapping succeeds, it attempts the already-proven compat signal/return test.

Built ORBW00032, “TEB Sentinel Signal Probe,” with `libc.prx` and
`libSceFios2.prx` staged. Package:
`pkgout/orbw00032-teb-sentinel/IV0000-ORBW00032_00-ORBW000320000000.pkg`,

this title for the next hardware check. The existing klog listener remains
running; no duplicate listener is needed.

ORBW00032 ran without crashing but could not test signal delivery: the PS4
rejected `mmap(MAP_FIXED)` at `0x1000` with `errno=22`. Its log result was
`SENTINEL_MAP_UNAVAILABLE`; this low-page mapping route is closed.

Built ORBW00033 as the high-address follow-up. It maps at `0x100001000` and
places the synthetic TCB at `0x100001e00`, so the qword at guest `FS:0x10` is
formed from `FiberData=0x1e00` (preserved) and `ArbitraryUserPointer=1` (changed).
This isolates whether libkernel accepts a high synthetic TCB while keeping the
non-fiber sentinel. Package:
`pkgout/orbw00033-teb-high-alias/IV0000-ORBW00033_00-ORBW000330000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `8492e3b168279afe24182ad14b8d9dad5ab8b921bc8faabf54fe3fe35ba5dcfc`.
Both required Sony modules are staged; uploaded to

launch ORBW00033. The klog listener remains active.

ORBW00033 also ran without crashing, but the PS4 rejected the fixed mapping at

at both low and high hints are therefore unavailable through this probe/SDK

ORBW00034 completed the synthetic-TCB signal round trip without crashing:

by the handler; it returned to native code with `returned_vector=1`. The

ORBW00035 tests whether a non-fixed `mmap` hint at `0x100001000` can return a

ORBW00036 repeats the hint test with a 16 KiB-aligned base hint

### ORBW00036: 16 KiB-aligned TEB sentinel mapping (2026-10-16)

ORBW00036 completed the signal round-trip with a synthetic TCB at
`0x100001e00`. An ordinary anonymous mapping with a 16 KiB-aligned hint at
`0x100000000` was honored exactly. This preserves the observed non-fiber
`TEB32.FiberData=0x1e00`; the overlapping `ArbitraryUserPointer` dword becomes
`1`. The klog result was `signals=2 unexpected=11 ... returned_vector=1`,
with guest FS base and xstate values recorded. This confirms compat signal
entry/return works with this high TCB layout, and narrows the remaining
question to whether changing `ArbitraryUserPointer` is safe for Wine's TEB
semantics. The probe did not crash.

### ORBW00037: repeat signal test on a worker thread (2026-10-16)

ORBW00037 moves the proven ORBW00036 signal/TCB test to a newly created pthread
worker. The main thread waits while the worker captures its own host FS base,
installs its own alternate signal stack, maps the high TCB, and runs the same
compat fault/return checks. This checks whether the successful path is valid
for ordinary per-thread execution, rather than relying on main-thread setup.

Built with `libc.prx` and `libSceFios2.prx` staged. Package:
`pkgout/orbw00037-worker-tcb/IV0000-ORBW00037_00-ORBW000370000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `04937274a03fe61c9fbead9de956380173f91a84f9df751f0402f0e8894d4685`.

ORBW00037 launched and completed on the PS4. The worker thread independently
captured host FS `0x880dac720`, installed its alternate signal stack and Sony
SIGSEGV callback, mapped the aligned synthetic TCB at `0x100001e00`, delivered
both signal events (`SIGTRAP` and expected compat `SIGSEGV`), restored native
execution, and preserved the test vector state (`returned_vector=1`). The
worker-thread path therefore works; the earlier successful path was not
specific to the process main thread. The title remained alive in its intended
wait loop after printing the result.

### ORBW00038: repeat the bridge test on a fresh worker (2026-10-16)

ORBW00038 runs the same test on two newly created pthreads, one after the
other. Each worker independently captures host FS, installs an alternate
signal stack and the Sony exception handler, maps the aligned high synthetic
TCB, enters compat mode, and checks signal return and vector preservation. It
unmaps its TCB page before exiting so the next worker must perform setup again.
This targets stale or main-thread-only state without introducing concurrent
access to the probe's intentionally shared diagnostic buffers.

Built with `libc.prx` and `libSceFios2.prx` staged. Package:
`pkgout/orbw00038-worker-repeat/IV0000-ORBW00038_00-ORBW000380000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `69428813a57c45c25c7d103c08c3df4475b663fea4ab7daa4a563a8c63347aa4`.

ORBW00038 launched and both worker cycles completed. Each worker independently
captured a different host FS base (`0x880dac720` and `0x880dac860`), then
installed its own alternate signal stack and completed the same compat
signal/return sequence. Both reported `signals=2 unexpected=11` and
`returned_vector=1`; both mapped the synthetic TCB at `0x100001e00` while
preserving the `FiberData=0x1e00` dword. This confirms the tested bridge setup
can be initialized repeatedly on fresh pthreads and does not depend on one
worker's host FS value.

### ORBW00039: place the signal TCB below 4 GiB (2026-10-16)

ORBW00039 tests the least invasive way around the TEB overlap: request an
ordinary anonymous mapping at the 16 KiB-aligned hint `0x10000000` and place
the signal TCB at `page+0x1e00`. When the returned TCB address is below 4 GiB,
the qword at guest `FS:0x10` has upper dword zero, preserving
`TEB32.ArbitraryUserPointer`; the probe then exercises signal delivery and
return on two fresh worker threads. If the allocator returns a high address,
the worker logs that fact and skips the compat fault rather than changing the
upper TEB dword.

Built with `libc.prx` and `libSceFios2.prx` staged. Package:
`pkgout/orbw00039-low-tcb-hint/IV0000-ORBW00039_00-ORBW000390000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `f1dbf06e0080ed2bd2b09cd3e3f81e2de5102dd65c574a0c082893eb23ca84d3`.

ORBW00039 launched and both fresh worker cycles passed. The ordinary mapping
hint `0x10000000` was honored exactly, so each synthetic signal TCB was placed
at `0x10001e00`, below 4 GiB. The guest `FS:0x10` qword therefore had
`FiberData=0x10001e00` and `ArbitraryUserPointer=0`; both compat signal
round-trips returned (`signals=2 unexpected=11`, `returned_vector=1`) with
independent worker host-FS bases. This is the first hardware result showing a
working signal TCB without borrowing the upper `ArbitraryUserPointer` dword.
The remaining semantic change is that a non-fiber thread's `FiberData` sentinel
`0x1e00` becomes a valid low pointer. Wine's `HasFiberData` flag remains false;
validate any native-backend integration against fiber conversion and DLL
loading before treating it as general support.

### ORBW00040: experimental native backend with per-thread low TCB (2026-10-16)

ORBW00039 established the low-address layout, so I added an experimental
per-thread mapping in `wow64native/unix.c`: `BTCpuThreadInit` maps a zeroed
16 KiB region below 4 GiB, writes `page+0x1e00` into the native thread's
`TEB32.FiberData`, leaves `ArbitraryUserPointer` untouched, and logs the old
and new values. `BTCpuThreadTerm` restores the previous FiberData when the game
has not replaced it, then unmaps the page. This is an opt-in native-backend
experiment; fiber conversion remains a known semantic risk and this is not a
general-support claim.

Patch: `src/prospero-win-patches-ps4/0035-wow64native-low-per-thread-signal-tcb.patch`.
The PS4 Unix companion rebuilt successfully and contains the new diagnostic
marker. The host PE `wow64native.dll` was rebuilt and explicitly staged. Built
relocatable title ORBW00040 so Oblivion's fixed image base remains available:
`pkgout/orbw00040-native-low-tcb-reloc/IV0000-ORBW00040_00-ORBW000400000000.pkg`,
SHA-256 `2f3612364cd85cc11ec7c932fa6740a5ba404dcd0b254318f745f3312c190612`.
Both the handoff EBOOT and relocated Wine PRX are ELF DYN. Package contains the
relocated app, both wow64native copies, and matching PRX companions. Hardware
validation is pending.

The first ORBW00040 launch selected the `oblivion-gog` profile with

The runtime heartbeat advanced, but `sink_calls` stopped at 877;
`frames_put=0`, `shown=0`, and audio stayed at zero. No
`PW_NATIVE_SIGNAL_TCB` message appeared. The title then exited from the PS4
menu with status 0, not a crash. Klog:
`build-ps4-logs/ps4-klog-20261016-063744.log`.

For the next run, I added `PW_NATIVE_STAGE` messages at entry, rejection,

The live-profile run loaded `Oblivion.exe` and stayed alive while the screen
remained hung. The first sampled thread (`TEB=0x7ffda000`) advanced from

then all counters and its TSC remained unchanged through 12:00:46. Over that
interval the process heartbeat continued, CPU time rose from 9.1 to 93.8
seconds, `sink_calls` crept from 892 to 929, and there were still no presented
frames or audio. At 12:00:47 klog records ORBW00040 being killed and the task
ending with `error=0`; the runtime logger disconnected at the same time. This
establishes that the sampled native thread stopped making WoW64 host
transitions while the process kept consuming CPU.
It does **not** establish that this is the game thread: patch 0035 watched only
the first native thread, so the aggregate CPU counter may belong to another
thread.

To resolve that ambiguity, patch
`src/prospero-win-patches-ps4/0036-wow64native-profile-all-threads.patch`
registers up to 64 native threads and samples each profile every two seconds,

#### ORBW00040 GTA IV comparison (2026-10-16)

After the Oblivion run, the user selected the `gtaiv-test` profile with the
same package and `cpu=native`. GTA IV also loaded its PE32 image at
`0x00400000`. Its registered native thread (`TEB=0x7ffda000`) advanced briefly,
then the transition counters and TSC stayed fixed at
`host_calls=1224`, `guest_calls=1225`, `unix_calls=52`, and
`syscall_calls=1172` from 12:11:12 through 12:12:14 (`changed=0`, 35 samples).
During that interval the process remained alive and accumulated CPU time up to
92.1 seconds, but `frames_put=0`, `shown=0`, and `audio=0`. The user closed the
app from the PS4 menu; klog records `BlockingKill` followed by
`Task ... ended (... error=0x0)`, so this was a clean close, not a title crash.

This reproduces the *shape* of Oblivion's native run: a registered native
thread stops crossing WoW64 boundaries while process CPU time continues, with
no presented frames or audio. It does not prove both games are stuck at the
same guest instruction, nor that the sampled TEB belongs to the CPU-consuming
thread. GTA IV therefore weakens the idea that Oblivion's fixed image
relocation alone explains this particular hang, but we still need a guest-PC
sample tied to each native thread before attributing the shared symptom to a
specific WoW64 or game path. Avoid another unchanged package run; the next
instrument should capture the interrupted RIP for registered native threads
without overwriting or bypassing Wine's signal handlers.

Patch `src/prospero-win-patches-ps4/0037-wow64native-profile-thread-cpu-time.patch`
adds per-thread CPU clocks to that watcher. Each `BTCpuThreadInit` registers
its `pthread_getcpuclockid()` clock; the watcher reports cumulative
`cpu_ns` and `cpu_delta_ns` beside the existing WoW64 transition counters.
This distinguishes a native thread spinning in guest code from one that has
stopped running while some other thread consumes the process CPU. The PS4
Unix companion rebuilt successfully, the patch reverse-applies cleanly to the
working tree, and the PRX contains the `version=3` diagnostic format.

The relocatable ORBW00040 package was rebuilt and uploaded to
`/data/pkg/IV0000-ORBW00040_00-ORBW000400000000.pkg` (GoldHEN FTP `226`):
